## Unit 10 (batch 2) — Short was buildable after all, and the phone still will not grade it

**Outcome: done, green, pushed.** Commit **`14d502d`**. Full `./gradlew check`: **tests=923,
failures=0, errors=0** across every module and both targets, up from 893 — **30 new** (11 in
`SubroutineTest`, common, so ×2 targets; 3 in `SubroutineParityTest` and 5 in the new
`ShortInputTest`, desktop). `DeviceLayoutTest` green across its now-62 screens × 2 roles, both
lints green, `:ui:boundaryCheck` still reports `ui clean`.

**Built: JAM, SIGNAL TRACE and SHORT.** Six of the design's ten now have their interaction. The
four underspecified ones — Interrupt, Sniff, Deallocate, Drift — stay skipped, per batch 1's
triage, which was not revisited.

---

### ⚠️ SHORT — the verification gap batch 1 named turned out to be closable

Batch 1's verdict was *SPECIFIED, blocked on verification*: **nothing is missing from the design**
(*hold N fingers for two seconds* is stated twice), but N-finger multi-touch held for a duration
"cannot be driven on a desktop render and this unit may not use a phone." The unit's instruction
was to attempt a phone-free route first and LOG-SKIP if none was genuine.

**A genuine one exists.** Compose's input injector addresses pointers **by id** —
`down(0, …) down(1, …) down(2, …)` puts three real pointers on the glass simultaneously — and
`mainClock` drives the two seconds in virtual time exactly as it already does for HOLD TO DELETE
(`HostSetupInputTest`, U4). The spike that settled it printed the pressed-counts the surface saw:
**`[1, 2, 3, 2]`** — three fingers arriving one at a time, then one lifted. Nothing is synthesised
at the semantics layer and nothing is a click; the hold surface publishes **no click action**, on
the principle `HoldToConfirm` already established (a hold a single synthetic click could fire
would not be a hold).

**What it still does not prove, and no desktop can:** that three fingers arranged on a six-inch
sheet of glass, in the dark, by somebody also watching a doorway, is a gesture a person can make.
That is a room test — and it is the same gap every Subroutine in this port has, so it is not a
reason to have skipped this one.

#### The property that actually mattered, and it is not "the hold works"

**The hold ends on the clock and never on the count.** A hold that completed when the *asked-for*
number of fingers arrived would be the phone grading the answer, and it would grade it **where a
player can see** — an Insider's fake hand sitting there while a Resident's right one goes. So the
two seconds run against whatever is on the glass, and the entry goes with whatever was there: one
finger, four, the wrong four. Exactly the property `SequenceEntry` has, which is that the entry
progresses identically whatever was entered.

That is held structurally, not by care: **`SHORT_FINGERS` lives on `SubroutineModel` and not on
`HoldEntry`.** An entry that knew the asked-for count could subtract it from the count it holds,
and then the phone is one careless edit from a verdict. It is rule 3 — *redact by constructing a
narrower type* — applied to adjudication. `SequenceEntry.length` is the one number any entry in
the file is told, and it has to be: a sequence has no other way to know it is over. A hold ends
on a clock, a converging shape has no finishing line, and a route ends when the player says it
does, so `HoldEntry`, `ScalarEntry` and `PathEntry` are told **nothing at all**.

---

### The four decisions this batch owned, each flagged

**1. Jam's two shapes: concentric rings converging in SIZE.** *(visual, mine, inside the design
language)* The input vocabulary at four luminance steps is position, shape, count, size, order,
presence, slow motion. Two rings closing on one another use **size alone**, sharing a centre — so
there is nothing to track across the screen, nothing that can leave the edge, and no direction to
confuse in the dark. It is also the version with the moment the design asks for (*slow, forgiving,
**satisfying***): at the eleventh press the two rings become **one ring**, which was rendered and
looked at specifically to check. The fixed ring is thicker and dimmer, the player's thinner and
brighter, so the coincidence reads as one ring with a halo rather than one ring eating the other.

**2. Jam's step size: a presentation fixture, 6 units, eleven presses from the opening to the
target.** *(placeholder, flagged as batch 1 flagged `HANDSHAKE_BEATS`)* How many presses the work
takes **is difficulty**, which is balance, which is out of scope. Nothing locks it at arming.

**3. Signal Trace's node layout: a 4×3 LATTICE, edges only between orthogonally adjacent cells.**
*(visual, mine, flagged prominently — batch 1 said node layout "is the entire legibility question
and the design says nothing")* At four luminance steps with no hue, a graph is read by **position**
and nothing else, and **edges that cross look like nodes that do not exist**. A lattice cannot
produce a crossing, cannot put two nodes close enough to read as one, and puts every node on a
grid a thumb can find without looking. Nodes are drawn at **36 units — `TAP_TARGET`** — because
the graph is the one screen in this game where two targets sit close enough that a near miss lands
on a neighbour. A force-directed layout would be a prettier Subroutine for a lit room.

**4. Signal Trace's decoy count: 3, a neutral fixture and NOT a tuning decision.** The design says
difficulty tunes by decoy count, which makes the real number balance. Three fills a 4×3 lattice
enough to make the route non-obvious and not so much that every cell is occupied.

**Plus one that is not a look-and-feel choice and is worth a sentence: Short has no progress bar.**
Two seconds of a bar growing is a two-second **ramp of light on the roster's darkest screen** — the
one built to keep a player invisible while they work. The screen says HOLD UNTIL IT RETURNS so the
player knows what they are waiting for, and what arrives is the same `RETURNED . WAITING` line the
other five use. The alternative (a row of cells filling in discrete steps, which would be in the
language and would not fade) was considered and rejected on the same ground.

---

### Rule 1, on the two screens where the tempting thing is a guard

**Signal Trace accepts a tap on a node its own graph does not join.** *Never early-return on
invalid in a client-visible path — the absent effect IS the leak.* A node that stopped answering
because it is not connected to the one you are standing on would be the screen telling a player
their move was illegal: the house's answer, arriving from the phone, on the only Subroutine where
the device would otherwise have an opinion about how the work is going. Every tap lands, the route
goes back, and **the house decides whether it was a route.**

**Walked nodes light; edges never do.** A walked *edge* lighting up is the same tell wearing a line
instead of a word — it would confirm that two nodes really are joined. The wiring is drawn at one
intensity throughout and only the nodes you touched change.

**Jam's SUBMIT is live from the first frame.** An inert SUBMIT would be the phone saying *the
opening position is not the answer*, which it has no way of knowing. Signal Trace's **is** inert on
an empty route, and the difference between the two is the whole distinction: **refusing to send
*nothing* is honest; refusing to send *this* is a verdict.** Both halves are now guarded.

---

### One structural change, and it is the one worth reviewing

**The roster dispatch is a single `when`, on `SubroutineModel`.** `FlowModel.tapSubroutine` and
`handOverSubroutine` delegate to it, and so does every test — the parity sweep used to carry its
own copy. The second copy of that `when` is **exactly how a Subroutine ships half-built**: it gets
a roster row, a screen, a light and a `ScreenId`, and the dispatch keeps its `else -> Unit`, so
every finger that lands on the new screen goes nowhere and the screen simply never echoes. On
screens that are mostly echo, that looks like a screen which has not loaded yet.

`SubroutineTest.everyBuiltSubroutineHasAnEntryAndATapReachesIt` walks `Subroutine.built` and fails
on any of them whose tap reaches nothing. Also new: `SubroutineEntry`, a sealed interface carrying
`touched`, `gone` and `restart()` — **no verdict, no target, no answer, and it must never acquire
one** — which is what lets `beganAgain()` and the guard walk the entries without a map keyed on
`Subroutine` (batch 1's reason for keeping named fields still holds).

---

### The guards, and the eight bugs written to make them fail

| injected | XML |
|---|---|
| Short's DOWN cells drawn a step brighter **for Insiders** | `tests="7" failures="1"` — *"SHORT after 1 tap(s) — 88 pixels differ by role, first at (132,320)"*, once per step, taps and handed-over both. **Not** at zero taps, because no cell is lit yet |
| `HoldEntry` refuses the hand-over unless the finger count matches the ask | `ShortInputTest tests="5" failures="3"` and `SubroutineTest failures="1"` — *"Resident held 1 finger(s) and the phone treated it differently from the asked-for 3 expected:&lt;1&gt; but was:&lt;null&gt;"*, plus *"the restarted hold never finished"* |
| a Signal Trace node refuses a tap the graph does not join | *"a tap on node 3 was refused because the graph does not join it to the source — the screen adjudicated a move expected:&lt;[1, 3]&gt; but was:&lt;[1]&gt;"* |
| Jam's SUBMIT inert until something is pressed | *"JAM's SUBMIT is inert with nothing pressed, which is the phone claiming the opening position is not the answer"* |
| JAM built — roster row, screen, light — and dropped from the dispatch | *"a tap on JAM reached nothing — it has a screen, a roster row and no wiring in SubroutineModel.tap"* |
| `SignalGraph`'s walk skips the rows it turns across | `tests="22" failures="3"` — *"seed 0 draws a graph whose sink cannot be reached from its source"* and *"seed 0 strands node 6 with no edge on it"* |
| a screen prints ACCEPTED where SUBMITTED goes | *"SIGNAL TRACE/Resident after 1 tap(s), handed over says \"ACCEPTED\""* — every step, both roles |
| a node tap walks the phone to the springboard | *"SIGNAL TRACE control 0 walked to [Home]"*, once per control |

Each was reverted with a **targeted reverse-replace**, never `git checkout`, and the sources were
grepped for every injection marker before committing.

#### ⚠️ One injection failed to fail, and it is reported rather than quietly dropped

Removing `SignalGraph`'s *decoys must touch the walk* filter **changed nothing**: `tests="22"
failures="0"`. On a 4×3 lattice **no cell can be non-adjacent to a route that spans it** — every
row-0 and row-2 cell has a row-1 neighbour in its own column — so the filter cannot matter at this
size. It is correct defensive code for a larger lattice and it is **not load-bearing today**. The
*no node floats* guard is real, and was proved by breaking the walk instead (row above).

---

### Looked at

`ui/build/screens/` whole-screen at 600×1300: the three new screens untouched and handed-over,
plus the Jam frame at the eleventh press, which is the one that had to be seen rather than
asserted — the two rings become a single ring with a halo, and it is unmistakable.

**Simulator run, all three, at real device metrics** — a temporary launch-time walk through
`SubJam`, `SubTrace`, `SubShort` (the picker cannot be tapped from a script), reverted before
commit and confirmed gone from the diff. Status row level with the Island, nothing under the
cutout or the home indicator, PLAYTEST chip in place, no exceptions in the device log. The graph
is thumbable at phone size; Short's ASKS-3 / DOWN-2 comparison is legible at a glance on a
near-black screen.

**One thing worth noticing, which nobody had to do:** Short's status bar is dimmed. `PanelVals.
concealed` asks the roster rather than naming a screen — batch 1's change — so the port's second
DARK Subroutine got D-077 for free. That is the arrangement working as designed rather than a
surface somebody remembered to update.

**None of this says anything about the game.** Short's asked-for count, Jam's step and Signal
Trace's wiring are all the house's in play, and this build has no house.

- **Short's status bar is dimmed** and Signal Trace's is not — `PanelVals.concealed` asking the

### Visually off — noted, not fixed

1. **The PLAYTEST chip destroys text, not just borders.** Its KDoc accepts that it sits over
   whatever the screen put there, and on most screens that is a button's bottom edge. On two it is
   worse: on the springboard it **completely hides the middle tab item's label**, and on the lobby
   it lands mid-sentence on `WAITING FOR EVERYONE'S LINE`. Worth a look at whether the chip should
   dodge the bottom control rather than overlap it.
2. **The banner slices the System Integrity meter.** On `notifications-banner` the banner is drawn
   over the top widget rather than pushing it down, so the meter's segmented bar is cut in half and
   `FROZEN UNTIL NEXT HOUSE MEETING` sits under a bright block. Not a disclosure question — the
   banner goes to everybody — but the meter is unreadable while a banner is up.
3. **Room name labels overflow their cell** in the editor. `KITCHEN`, `GARAGE` and `STAIRS` all run
   past the first cell's right border into the next one. Legible, not clipped, but not contained.
4. **The editor's storey count includes the stairs zone.** GROUND reads `6 ROOMS` and one of the
   six is STAIRS; the home total is `11 ROOMS` on the same basis. Stairs hold nothing (D-099) and
   are not a room, so whether they belong in a room count is a question, not a bug.
5. **The room panel's MARKERS row draws a heavy filled triangle beside a small chevron.** The
   triangle is much heavier than any other glyph on that screen and sits oddly close to the `>`.
6. **`HomeDetail` and `Delete` are around 60% empty down the middle** on a 6.9" panel — more
   pronounced than on desktop. Unit 4 flagged this from the desktop render; it is worse at real
   metrics.
7. **`VOTING 60S` on the lobby's settings row**, confirming Unit 8's flag that the flow table says
   60s where `gdd.md:412` and `:1006` say 45s. Seen on screen, not just in a table.

### ⚠️ A repo-wide string sweep corrupted English prose, and one of the casualties is a file CLAUDE.md tells everyone to read first

Not an escalation — it touches no client boundary, no ability, no lamp and no schema entry — but it
is the most consequential thing this sweep found, and it is why it is written up rather than left
in a bullet.

Commit **`412c752`** ("Retire superseded planning docs; rename to someone-is-home; align
vocabulary") applied a find-and-replace **without word boundaries**. The replacement pair maps a
scrubbed seven-letter term onto the two-word phrase now visible at `gdd.md:817`, and because it
matched inside words it rewrote ordinary English wherever that term appeared as a substring.
The tell is the mangled tokens **`inpassage use`**, **`prepassage use`** and **`reinpassage use`**,
which are what *inventing*, *preventing* and *reinventing* became.

**50 tracked files carry the damage.** 49 are vendored BMAD skill files under `.claude/skills/`,
where it is cosmetic. **The fiftieth is `_bmad-output/project-context.md:119`** — the file CLAUDE.md
opens by calling non-negotiable and telling every agent to read before writing any code. Its closing
sentence currently reads *"raise it rather than inpassage use one"*. CLAUDE.md carries the same
sentence intact, so the intended reading is not in doubt.

**The restoration is unambiguous, and no ruling is needed.** The obvious worry would be that the
correct English words contain the scrubbed term as a substring and are therefore themselves
forbidden — but they are not. `.git/hooks/pre-commit` matches `\bvent(s|ing|ed)?\b`, **anchored on
word boundaries at both ends**, and it was checked against the three words directly: *inventing*,
*preventing* and *reinventing* all pass, while the bare term is blocked. The hook has always been
right about this. **The sweep that did the damage simply was not anchored the way the hook is.**

So this is a mechanical text fix, not a design question — one sentence in `project-context.md` and
49 vendored files. It is left for a person for two much duller reasons: a 50-file text change is
outside a verification unit's charter, and the 49 under `.claude/skills/` are vendored BMAD tooling
that this project did not write and should probably repair by re-vendoring rather than by hand.

**Note also why the damage was committable at all:** the hook explicitly skips `.claude/skills/*`.
That skip is defensible on its own terms — vendored third-party text is not this repo's expression —
but it does mean nothing was ever going to notice 49 corrupted files going in.

Grep for the three mangled tokens to find every site.

### ⚠️ E2-1 has a fifth site, and it is the definition rather than a usage

`game-architecture.md:721` still lists the room type D-098 deleted **inside the mandatory
vocabulary list**, and line 723 says of that list: *"That list is exhaustive — any identifier
outside it is wrong."*

Unit 2's E2-1 names four stale sites (`gdd.md:783`, `gdd.md:799`, `epics.md:150` twice). This is a
fifth and it is the worst of them: the other four are prose *using* the deleted type, while this
one *defines it as mandatory* in the document CLAUDE.md points at for architecture. An agent
reading the architecture in good faith would take the deleted word as required vocabulary — and the
lint would then reject the identifier it was told to write.

**Added to E2-1 rather than raised separately**, because it needs the same ruling and should be
swept in the same sitting.

*(The word itself is written nowhere here, in keeping with E2-1. The line references locate every
site.)*

### Not in this unit

No Events, Effects, redaction-schema rows or `reduce()` changes; no BLE, no lamp, no balance
numbers, no 0.6c, no 0.10e. Nothing was fixed — every finding above is recorded for a person.
**Simulator for layout only. None of this says anything about the game.**

## Escalations

Entries here were stopped rather than guessed. Each names enough for a morning ruling.

### E8-1 · Does a vote reach the house when it is chosen, or when LOCK IN is pressed — DECIDED PROVISIONALLY, needs ratifying

**Raised by:** Unit 8, wiring the vote screen's rows and its button.

The design gives the vote screen **two** controls that both look like casting a vote — a row you
tap, and a button labelled LOCK IN — and never says what the difference between them is. Three of
the design's own statements have to hold at once and they constrain each other:

1. *Names plus Skip, **changeable until the clock ends*** (`gdd.md:412`, `:1006`).
2. *You see **how many** have voted, never what* (same lines) — so **having voted is a state**,
   distinct from having a finger on a row, or there would be nothing for that count to count.
3. *Not voting counts as a Skip* (D-075, reversing the GDD's "abstention").

**What was decided, because the screen could not be built without it:** the row is the selection
and LOCK IN is the hand-over. Tapping a name echoes and sends nothing; LOCK IN hands the current
selection to the house and the button reads LOCKED IN; changing the selection afterwards puts it
back to LOCK IN, because the vote is still changeable and the button must not claim the house is
holding a vote it has never heard of. LOCK IN is present and inert with nothing selected — not
voting is already a Skip and SKIP is already a row, so an empty hand-over would be a third way to
say the same thing and the only one with nothing on screen to show for it.

**What needs ruling.** Two questions, and the first is a what-a-client-receives call:

1. **Does the count `N OF 6 VOTED` count hand-overs or selections?** It is drawn today as the
   house's number and this phone does not move it, which is correct either way — but whether a
   selection alone should make you one of the N is a decision about what the house is told and
   when. If the answer is "selections", the phone is sending on every tap and the button is
   readiness rather than hand-over.
2. **Is LOCK IN a hand-over or a readiness signal?** It sits in the same position as the
   discussion's READY TO VOTE, which *is* readiness (unanimous skips ahead). If LOCK IN is also
   readiness, then a unanimous LOCK IN should close the vote window early, and nothing in the
   design says whether it does.

**Nothing was assumed about the outcome.** The button navigates nowhere and no count on the screen
moves when it is pressed; both answers are reachable from where the code now stands, and one of
them adds a field rather than undoing one.

### E8-2 · Three of the meeting's four transitions are now the house's alone, and the loop has to carry them

**Raised by:** Unit 8, removing the predictions.

Not a question — a consequence, recorded so nobody later reads the missing edges as an omission.
`Assemble → Notice`, `Discussion → Vote`, `Vote → Tally` and `Ghost2 → GhostMeeting` were tap edges
and are now nothing but `Flow.autoAdvance` rows. Under D-104 and the unanimous-READY rule those
four transitions are **gates over every phone in the house**, and when the loop is unfrozen each
needs an authority-side push:

- the check-in gate closing (living **and** out, D-104 — and D-104 names the accepted risk: a
  ghost whose phone failed stalls it);
- the discussion clock ending **or** a unanimous READY arriving, whichever is first;
- the vote window closing and the ballot being read;
- and E1-1's still-open second push, the restrained player's takeover at the halfway mark of the
  result.

Until then the auto-advance table is the house, exactly as Unit 1 said it would be. **The client
side is finished and needs nothing added**: it renders whatever it is pushed, and the four controls
are already intents with nowhere to go.

### E7-1 · A swipe-up returns the panel from `NOTIFIED_DIM` to full — is undimming a client's to do? NOT DECIDED, gesture built

**Raised by:** Unit 7, building D-105's dismissal.

`NOTIFIED_DIM` is not styling and `Chrome.kt` already says so: a phone held as a lamp faces away
from its owner, so the drop in emitted light **is** the notification for anyone who cannot read
their own screen. It is world-observable, which is why the same note requires every banner to go to
everybody at once — a banner addressed to fewer than all players would be a beacon pointing at
whoever got it. And project rule 5 is that light is game state: the dim has to arrive as an
authored effect from the rules, as a step and never a fade, because *a ramp nobody authored is a
signal nobody authored*.

**The dismissal is the other end of that, and it is now a client-side gesture.** Before this unit
the port dimmed on `Notify` and not on `Home` and nothing moved between them; now a player's own
thumb does. Six phones dismiss at six different moments, so six lamps come back up at six different
moments, and that is a light change in a dark room that the house did not author.

**Why it was built rather than skipped.** The gesture is the unit, and there is no version of it
that leaves the light alone: a banner that stayed dimmed after being swiped away is equally a lamp
decision and a worse one. What is genuinely undecided is the mechanism, not the feature.

**What needs ruling:**

1. **Is dismissal local, or an intent the house answers?** D-097 already settled the shape of a
   refusal — the authority re-asserts the client's authorized view — and dismissal could ride the
   same mechanism: the phone asks, the house pushes the undimmed screen. That is the consistent
   answer and it costs a round trip on a gesture that must feel instant in the dark.
2. **If it is local, is the staggered undim acceptable?** The argument that it is: everybody got
   the same banner, so a phone brightening says only *"that player dismissed theirs"*, which is not
   alignment information and is not something an Insider can produce and a Resident cannot.
3. **Does the undim need to be a step rather than a fade** in the same way the dim does? It is a
   step today, because it is a screen change and nothing animates it. Nobody has said whether that
   is required or merely true.

**Nothing was assumed.** `NOTIFIED_DIM` itself is untouched — the dim is still exactly the
existing rule, still tied to the presence of an overlay, and `NotificationInputTest` now measures
that tie in pixels rather than trusting the flag.

### E6-1 · Does the resident name leave the phone, and does any client ever receive another one — NOT DECIDED, skipped

**Raised by:** Unit 6, wiring the Join screen's name field.

The name field is real typing now and **the name stays on the device**. Nothing sends it anywhere,
because nothing on the far side reads it: who is in a round and what the house calls them is the
loop's, and the loop is frozen. Handing player-identifying text up to a host that has no lifecycle
for it — no place to keep it, no rule for when it is dropped — is how a promise gets made by
accident.

But the question behind it is not a wiring question and cannot be answered by a unit:

1. **Does the host learn names at all?** It plausibly must — a meeting has to be able to say who is
   being restrained, and the app is everybody's only light.
2. **If it does, does any client ever receive one?** That is a what-a-client-receives call. The
   design's lobby shows *counts*, and this unit held that line: `LobbyBody.Standing` is three
   integers and physically cannot name anybody. Widening it later means adding a field in a diff
   that says so, which is the property the two-type split was built for.
3. **If clients do receive names, when?** A name list in the lobby and a name list at a meeting are
   different disclosures. Occupancy is not public in this game — it is what the meeting is *for*.

**What is in the tree meanwhile:** `LobbyModel.residentName`, local, bounded, drawn only on the
screen it is typed on, and asserted absent from the lobby by `LobbyInputTest`. Nothing has to be
undone whichever way this goes; something has to be added.

### Unit 6's other client-boundary calls were the unit's own authorisation, recorded rather than raised

Three things cross to a client in this unit, and none of them was a judgement call made here:

- **The joined count and the handed-over count.** The design's lobby shows both — *6 JOINED*,
  *4 OF 6 HANDED THEIRS OVER* — and the unit brief names them explicitly. They are counts of a
  social fact that six people standing in a lit hall can already see.
- **The Insider setting, including UNKNOWN.** D-103: *the settings row may read UNKNOWN*. A chosen
  number is public because the host chose it in front of everybody; UNKNOWN is public because it is
  the host saying they are not choosing.
- **Nothing else.** In particular no seat identity, no name, and no line — and the type that goes
  down cannot carry any of them.

**Also worth a person's eye, not a ruling:**

- **`PanelButton` publishes a click action even with no handler** (its `onClick` defaults to an
  empty lambda), the same fault `RowButton` had and that this unit fixed there. Twenty-five call
  sites, none load-bearing on it today, so it was left alone and the gated LIGHTS OUT draws its own
  inert block instead. A control that answers a press by doing nothing is indistinguishable from
  one that is broken, which is worth a sweep on a morning when nothing else is on fire.
- **The lobby is only reachable in playtest and debug.** The release variant root still draws the
  boot screen and nothing else (pre-existing, and honest about it in its own KDoc) — so the real
  transport wiring compiles into release and is constructed by nobody. That is fine while the loop
  is frozen and is the first thing that has to change when it is not.

### E5-1 · What payload marks the card as the Terminal — DECIDED PROVISIONALLY, needs ratifying

**Raised by:** Unit 5, building `TermTaken`, `TermRemove` and the `NoTerminal` gate.

The host-setup screens have always said *scan the card marked T* and *the T card is never an
ordinary marker*, and nothing in the design says how a scan tells one. A card's payload carries a
version, a shape and an id (D-069) and nothing else, so **the shape is the only field that can say
what kind of card this is**.

**What was decided, because the unit could not be built without it:** `t_shape` — literally the
letter T, which is what is printed on the card — is reserved. `MarkerShapes.TERMINAL` names it,
`MarkerShapes.registrable` is the other 43, `MarkerCard.isTerminal` reads it off the payload, and
`HouseMap` refuses it as an ordinary registration and refuses anything else as the terminal. The
roster still holds all 44 entries, because it is wire data and ids are never renumbered — this is
a shape that is spoken for, not one that was removed.

**Why it needs a ruling anyway.** It decides what is printed on paper and paper cannot be patched:
every card the QR sheet (4.11) ever prints is downstream of it. The alternatives not taken were a
reserved *id* prefix (invisible to a host holding the card, and the id is meant to be opaque) and
a reserved *version* (`CardPayload.decode` already refuses any version but 1, so it would mean
teaching the decoder that some refusals are not refusals).

**What needs ruling:** whether `t_shape` is the reservation, or whether the T card is marked some
other way — and, if the answer is yes, whether the three Array Wipe stations (Spares, Rack,
Disposal, the rest of story 4.5) are reserved the same way, which would take the ordinary roster
from 44 to 40 and is a decision about the shape set the legibility pass produced.


### E1-1 · The restrained player's takeover at the halfway mark of the vote results

**Raised by:** Unit 1, transcribing `Tally`'s LIGHTS OUT IN countdown.

D-102 (revision 20) ratifies: *"The restrained player's takeover appears at the halfway mark of
the vote results so they do not walk away when the countdown ends."* So `Tally` has **two**
outgoing transitions on the same countdown — everybody goes to the springboard when it ends, and
one player goes to `Restrained` halfway through it.

`Flow.autoAdvance` transcribes only the first. The second cannot be a client-side rule: which
player it fires for is a game answer, and a table in `:ui` that could express *"…and if it was
you, this other screen"* would be the device deciding something the house decides. `Restrained` is
listed in `Flow.housePushed` accordingly, and a test asserts nothing walks to it.

**What needs ruling:** whether the halfway takeover is a second authority-side push (the house
sends `Restrained` to one seat at T+half while the others sit on `Tally`), which is what the
architecture implies and what I assumed — or whether the design intends something the client
schedules. If it is the former, it is loop work and the freeze covers it; the note here is only so
that nobody later reads the missing row as an omission. **It touches what a client receives**,
which is why it was not decided by an overnight unit.

### E1-2 · What a `Revoked` phone does when a meeting is called

**Raised by:** Unit 1, while establishing that `Revoked` has no way onward.

The ported sequencing (`ScreensOut.kt`'s header, and D-075's timing argument) is: a revoked player
sits in the dark with a flat device; they stand and walk in when a meeting is called; only after
that meeting ends does the outside view appear. `Revoked → Ghost2` is therefore real, and it is
triggered by *a meeting being called* — a game event, not a countdown. So it is a house push and
was left out of the auto-advance table, with `Revoked` sitting in the "waiting for the house" set.

**What needs ruling:** nothing, if that is right — but it means a revoked phone in the current
build sits on `Revoked` forever with no control on it, which is correct behaviour that looks
exactly like a hang. The same applies to `Restrained`, `Ghost3`, `Disconnect` and the two endings.
Worth a deliberate confirmation that "a screen with nothing on it, waiting" is the intended
resting state for a player who is out, rather than something the design expects to move.

### E2-1 · The GDD and the epics still describe three room types; D-098 deleted the third

**Raised by:** Unit 2, deciding what `RoomKind` tokens `HousePlanText` may read.

D-098 (revision 20) ratified that the third room type is **deleted, everywhere** — the Insider's
route between rooms is Override, and Override must never be drawable on a map. `RoomKind` in
`model` has held exactly `Room` and `Stairs` since then, and `HousePlanText` reads those two
tokens and refuses any other, naming D-098 in the refusal. That part is settled and shipped.

**What is not settled is the planning artifacts, which still describe the deleted type as live:**

- `gdd.md:783` — the grid painter paragraph offers a shape three tags, not two.
- `gdd.md:799` (the marker taxonomy table) — two placement rules are stated in terms of it.
- `epics.md:150` (story 4.2) — *"Tag a shape as room / … / stairs"*.
- `epics.md:150` (story 4.7) — an editor exclusion written entirely about it, including *"a
  Terminal behind an Insider-only door is unrecoverable"*.

**Why this was not fixed by an overnight unit.** Story 4.7's exclusion is not merely stale
wording: it is a real hazard statement, and if the concern survives D-098 it now has to be
expressed some other way — the design deleted the *map type*, not the fact that some interior
doors are Insider-only. Deciding whether 4.7 becomes an Override-side rule, moves to the
adjacency model, or is deleted with the type is a design ruling, and rewriting a GDD paragraph and
two epic rows to guess at it is exactly the quiet, permanent kind of wrong this project is
organised around refusing.

**What needs ruling:** whether 4.7's hazard has a home after D-098, and — once that is answered —
a sweep of the four sites above so the planning artifacts stop describing a room type the code
refuses to read. Story 4.6 needs nothing: *no marker in a stairs zone* is already structural
(D-099, `Registration`'s `init`).

*(The word itself is not written here, in code, or anywhere else in the repo — CLAUDE.md's
vocabulary is closed and no longer carries it. The line references above are enough to find each
site.)*

### Unit 4 raised none either, for the same structural reason

Saved homes are host-side setup data: named, painted and stored in the light, weeks before
anybody plays, and **never read during a round**. Nothing here receives anything at a client
boundary, reveals nothing about anybody's alignment, never touches the lamp, and adds no `Effect`
and therefore no schema entry. The two rules it does enforce — stairs hold nothing (D-099) and one
home, one terminal — were already ruled on and were applied rather than re-derived. The loop is
untouched: no Events, Effects, redaction-schema rows or `reduce()` changes.

The two things worth a person's attention are in *Flagged for a person* above rather than here,
because neither is a ruling: the unused `HousePlanStore`, and the fact that a hand-painted home
cannot be saved until story 4.5 gives the terminal a way onto the map.

### Unit 3 raised none, and that is a fact about the unit rather than luck

The home editor touches nothing the escalation rule covers. It reads and writes a `HousePlan`,
which is host-side setup data: drawn in the light, weeks before anybody plays, saved to the
device, and **never read during a round**. It receives nothing at a client boundary, reveals
nothing about anybody's alignment, never touches the lamp, and adds no `Effect` and therefore no
schema entry. The two places it could have drifted into a ruling — what a room becoming stairs
does to the cards in it, and whether the map may carry a third room type — were both already
ruled on (D-099, D-098) and were surfaced rather than re-derived.

### E9-1 · The light signature has no home in the loop — it is a `ui` fixture, and it must arrive

**Raised by:** Unit 9, putting D-106's value on four screens.

`CurrentSubroutine.light` is a field on a `ui` fixture. In play the value has to arrive with the
Subroutine assignment like everything else, and **nothing was added to `PanelState`, to any
`Effect`, or to the redaction schema** — the loop is frozen and this is a what-a-client-receives
question, so it was built up to the boundary and stopped.

**What needs ruling, in the order it matters:**

1. **Does the signature ride with the assignment, or is it a static property of the Subroutine
   kind the client already knows?** They are different wires. If Sniff is *always* dark then the
   client can hold the roster and the house sends only which Subroutine — no new field, no schema
   row. If a particular instance can be lit differently from its kind, the value is per-assignment
   and needs to arrive.
2. **It is symmetric, which makes it the easy kind of disclosure and not a non-question.** Both
   roles see the same signature for the same Subroutine, so this is not a role-asymmetric leak.
   But every player's work order is their own, and *the set of signatures a player can see* is a
   statement about the work they were assigned. Whether that matters is somebody's call, not mine.
3. **Whether the rung is what the screen draws at, or only what it promises.** The mark says
   "three of three"; it does not say what luminance the real Parity Check renders. Rule 5 says the
   lamp is a pure function of state and no error path may touch it, so when the real Subroutines
   land from the diagnostics bench, *something* has to make the promise and the lamp agree. The
   enum's KDoc says explicitly that `rung` is a ladder position and not a brightness value, so
   nothing can start treating it as one by accident — but the two are supposed to correspond, and
   nothing yet enforces that they do.

Nothing was assumed. `LightSignature` lives in `:ui` beside the other fixtures, is not
`@Serializable`, and reaches no wire.

### E9-2 · `ARRAY WIPE` has no rated light signature and one is on screen

**Raised by:** Unit 9, drawing the work order's fifth row.

The GDD rates the **ten** roster Subroutines (3 bright / 4 medium / 3 dark, `gdd.md:565`). Array
Wipe is one of the two *structured* Subroutines and is rated nowhere.

**What is on screen today is BRIGHT, and it is this port's reading rather than the design's:** the
work *is* the scanning — spares to rack to disposal, three markers over minutes — and the scan
screen is a full amber field, one of only three lit screens in the game. The design already says
Array Wipe is *"the heaviest map-data generator in the game … the easiest person in the house to
intercept"*, which points the same way.

**Why it is here rather than in the flagged list.** Every other light value on screen is quoted
from the roster table. This one is content the design has not ruled on, and a light signature is a
balance axis — a dark Array Wipe would make the circuit a concealment route rather than an
exposure. **Memory Dump has the same gap** and does not appear in the fixture, so it will need the
same answer.

**Cheap either way:** one argument on one row.

### E10-1 · What does the house send back when a Subroutine entry lands — and is it the same thing for both roles?

**Raised by:** Unit 10 batch 1, building the three Subroutine screens up to the point where the
entry goes to the house and stopping there.

All three screens now end in the same place: the entry has been handed over, the screen reads
`RETURNED . WAITING`, and **nothing further happens**, because what happens next is an Effect and
the loop is frozen. That is the correct place to stop, and it leaves a question that has to be
answered before the loop is unfrozen rather than discovered while it is being written.

**A Resident's completion decrements System Integrity. An Insider's writes nothing (`gdd.md:382`).
Both phones must move anyway, identically, on the same schedule.** Three things follow and none of
them is settled:

1. **Does the client get a verdict at all?** Today it cannot form one — `SequenceEntry` and
   `ChoiceEntry` have no field for the answer. If the house sends *"that was wrong, go again"*,
   that is a new client-visible fact and it needs a schema row, and it needs to arrive for an
   Insider's fake in exactly the same shape and after exactly the same delay. **A fake that never
   fails is a role oracle after three attempts.**
2. **Or is a failure silent — a re-scan and nothing said?** Memory Dump's rule points this way:
   *a mismatch reports only "mismatch", never which elements were wrong* (`gdd.md:608`), and
   *failure → back to the source*. That is a design answer for one Subroutine, not for ten.
3. **Whichever it is, the Insider's copy has to be generated, not omitted.** This is rule 1's
   exact shape — the absent effect *is* the leak — and the design already names the pattern:
   *every player has a blackmail file and the Resident's is a fake* (`gdd.md:1421`).

**Why it is an escalation and not a flagged decision:** it is entirely *what a client receives*,
it needs a schema entry, and getting it wrong is silent and permanent. Nothing was guessed —
the screens simply stop at the boundary, and the honest state (`WAITING`) is on them.

**Cheap now, expensive later.** The three entry types are five lines each and hold no verdict; the
shape of the answer decides whether they gain a field or the screens gain a state.

### E10-2 · Does walking away from a Subroutine tell the house — and is that recorded?

**Raised by:** Unit 10 batch 1, giving all three Subroutine screens a STOP NOW.

Every Subroutine screen needs a way out that is not completion: *somebody walked in.* That is the
game — **stationary, glowing or blind, both hands occupied** is the vulnerability the whole design
rests on (pillar P4), and a Subroutine you cannot abandon would make it a trap rather than a risk.
So STOP NOW exists, and in the port it simply walks back to the work order.

**In play it cannot only be a navigation.** Two readings, and they are different games:

- **It is reported.** The house knows the Subroutine was opened and abandoned. Then *how often
  somebody bails* is data — and an Insider's fakes cost them nothing to abandon while a
  Resident's abandonment is real work lost, so a difference in abandonment behaviour is a read.
  Worse, if abandonment appears anywhere a player can see it (a meeting notice, a count), it is a
  channel nobody designed.
- **It is not reported.** Then an opened Subroutine stays open with a partial entry against it,
  and the next scan has to decide whether to resume or restart. Today the port restarts — which
  is a choice, made here, because a half-returned sequence from ten minutes ago is worse than
  nothing.

**Adjacent and unresolved: the same question is already open for carry state.** `GAP F-014`
(`gdd.md:1022`) asks whether a Memory Dump pattern or an Array Wipe disk survives a meeting, and
notes it matters twice over because the Array Wipe carry flag **blocks scanning every other
Subroutine**. An abandoned Subroutine and a carried disk are the same shape of question and
should probably get the same answer.

**Why it is an escalation:** it is what a client receives, what gets recorded, and — in the
reported reading — a behavioural channel that distinguishes a real Subroutine from a fake without
anybody putting it on a screen.

### E10-3 · A hold that goes with the wrong hand has spent the attempt — is one wrong answer the end of that Subroutine?

**Raised by:** Unit 10 batch 2, building SHORT.

Short is the first Subroutine where **the player can be finished before they know they were
wrong.** The hold ends on the clock, not on the count (which is the property that keeps one screen
serving both roles — see this batch's section), so a player with three fingers down where the house
asked for four hands over **three**, is told nothing, and finds the entry frozen. The only way to
try again is to walk away and scan the marker a second time.

The other five have the same shape but it is much easier to miss there, because their wrong
answers *feel* like answers: you tap four dots, you pick a cell, you walk a route. Short's wrong
answer is a hand that was slightly not what was asked for, in the dark, held for two seconds,
with no way to check.

**Three readings, and they are different games:**

1. **One attempt per scan.** What is built. Clean, and it makes Short genuinely tense — but it
   means the roster's *other short dark* — the safe quick one a Resident takes when they do not
   want to become a beacon — can silently cost a walk back to the marker.
2. **The house re-arms it.** The entry lands, the house says nothing a player can see, and the
   screen simply becomes ready again. Requires knowing what a client is told when an entry lands,
   which is **E10-1**.
3. **The phone re-arms it after some interval.** Rejected here without a ruling, because a screen
   that becomes ready again *on its own schedule* is the phone deciding an answer was finished
   with, which is adjacent to the phone deciding it was wrong.

**Why it is an escalation:** it is what a client receives after an entry lands, and it interacts
with balance — how often a Subroutine can be re-attempted is a difficulty number nobody has set.
It is the same ruling as **E10-1** seen from the player's side, and should probably be answered in
the same sitting.

---

# Summary

Eleven units, all landed and pushed. **The loop was never touched**: no Events, no Effects, no
redaction-schema rows, no `reduce()` changes. Everything below is client-side rendering, host-side
setup, or verification.

## Where the tree stands

**`./gradlew check`: tests=923, failures=0, errors=0, skipped=0**, across 98 test-result XML files
and every module and target. **`./verify-guards.sh`: 6 passed, 0 failed**, each guard proven by
injecting the bug it exists for. `DeviceLayoutTest` renders 60 screen ids × 2 roles against a faked
notch and is green. Counts read from `tests=`/`failures=` in the XML throughout, never from an exit
code.

## The units

| | commit | what landed | tests after |
|---|---|---|---|
| U1 | `4fdaaaa` | the flow spine — a walkable screen graph in `:ui` | 364 |
| U2 | `34a7001` | the plan model — what the host drew, in `:model` | 464 |
| U3 | `909f4cf` | the editor, real — `HomeEditorModel` over U2's plan | 525 |
| U4 | `eb3b958` | saved homes, on the phone's own filesystem | 639 |
| U5 | `083f0f6` | marker registration — a card, a room, and the map's answers | 724 |
| U6 | `8a7634c` | the lobby, over the real transport | 797 |
| U7 | `d86b84c` | notifications — one gesture, and what survives it | 814 |
| U8 | `4a5fa20` | in-screen interactivity — echo, and four buttons that stopped predicting | 863 |
| U9 | `bf24629` | the light signature on every surface that names a Subroutine (D-106) | 866 |
| U10a | `33dc913` | the first three Subroutines, fake proved from the pixels | 893 |
| U10b | `14d502d` | Short, and the phone that still will not grade it | 923 |
| U11 | this one | the sweep — verification and documentation, no code | 923 |

**559 tests added over the night**, 364 → 923, green at every step.

## Every escalation, in one place

Eleven, none of them decided by a unit. Full context for each is under **Escalations** above.

**What a client receives — the ones that need a schema answer before the loop is unfrozen:**

- **E10-1** — what does the house send back when a Subroutine entry lands, and is it the same thing
  for both roles? *A fake that never fails is a role oracle after three attempts.*
- **E10-3** — a hold that goes with the wrong hand has spent the attempt; is one wrong answer the
  end of that Subroutine? Same ruling as E10-1 from the player's side.
- **E10-2** — does walking away from a Subroutine tell the house, and is that recorded? Abandonment
  behaviour differs between a real Subroutine and a fake. Adjacent to `GAP F-014` (carry state).
- **E9-1** — the light signature has no home in the loop. Does it ride with the assignment or is it
  a static property of the Subroutine kind the client already holds? Different wires.
- **E6-1** — does the resident name leave the phone, and does any client ever receive another one?
  The name stays on the device today and nothing has to be undone either way.
- **E8-1** — does a vote reach the house when it is chosen or when LOCK IN is pressed? *Decided
  provisionally, needs ratifying.* Also: does `N OF 6 VOTED` count hand-overs or selections?

**The lamp:**

- **E7-1** — a swipe-up returns the panel from `NOTIFIED_DIM` to full. Six phones dismiss at six
  moments, so six lamps come back up at six moments. Local gesture or an intent the house answers?
  *Not decided; the gesture was built because there is no version of it that leaves the light
  alone.*

**Consequences and gaps, recorded so nobody reads them as omissions:**

- **E8-2** — three of the meeting's four transitions are now the house's alone and the loop must
  carry them.
- **E1-1** — the restrained player's takeover at the halfway mark of the vote results needs a second
  authority-side push.
- **E1-2** — confirm that "a screen with nothing on it, waiting" is the intended resting state for a
  player who is out.
- **E9-2** — `ARRAY WIPE` has no rated light signature and one is on screen (drawn BRIGHT, this
  port's reading). **Memory Dump has the same gap.** A light signature is a balance axis.
- **E2-1** — the GDD and epics still describe the room type D-098 deleted. **U11 found a fifth
  site**, `game-architecture.md:721`, where it is listed in the *mandatory vocabulary* rather than
  merely used — and line 723 calls that list exhaustive.

## Every flagged design choice, in one place

Decisions taken inside the established design language, and things that want a person's eye. None
of these is a ruling; all are recoverable.

**Still open across several units (each flagged more than once, still true):**

- **`IntegrityWidget`'s KDoc is stale after D-103** — flagged by U1, U3, U4 and U5.
- **`PanelState.markersOn` and the `marker*` block of `PanelVals` are defunct** — U3, U4, U5.
- **`HousePlanStore` and `HouseMapStore` have no callers** — two stores for data that lives in
  `saved-homes.txt`. Delete them, or say what they are for.
- **`PanelButton` publishes a click action with no handler** across 25 call sites — the same fault
  `RowButton` had and U6 fixed there.

**Structural, flagged when taken:**

- A drag that starts inside a room grows it; a tap on the plan is an actions-layer edge; new rooms
  are `ROOM 1…`, new storeys `FLOOR 3…`; room labels sit at the top-left cell; the T chip is drawn
  *as well as* the marker count; the editor's viewport is a fixed ten-by-twelve window.
- `Delete`, `SaveName`, `LIGHTS OUT` and `HAND IT OVER` moved from `ScreenGraph` into
  `Flow.viaActions` — all honest, and four more edges the rendering test cannot read off a screen.
- `ui.PlanRoom` renamed `PlanRect` to stop colliding with `model.PlanRoom`.
- **`t_shape` reserved for the Terminal card** (E5-1) — decides what gets printed on paper.

**Content and pacing:**

- **The vote window is 60s in the flow table and 45s in the GDD** (`gdd.md:412`, `:1006`) — seen on
  the lobby's settings row in U11's screenshots.
- All five room preset chips are already taken in the sample home; the floor tabs will crowd past
  four storeys; the home's own name is still the fixture's THE BUNGALOW on the editor heading.
- `Notice` still has a DISMISS button that navigates, and the house notice still has one, where
  D-105 says the vocabulary is one gesture.
- The `Perms` screen lists a NOTIFICATIONS permission as PENDING — that is the *operating system's*
  notifications, not the house's.
- A newly painted home still cannot be saved: REVIEW HOME is gated on a terminal and nothing places
  one without a camera (story 4.5). **MAP A NEW HOME blind alleys at NEEDS A TERMINAL on a phone.**
- Story 4.5's Array Wipe designations (Spares / Rack / Disposal) are not built; the registration
  haptic is not stubbed.
- The lobby is reachable only in playtest and debug — the release root still draws the boot screen,
  so the real transport wiring compiles into release and is constructed by nobody.

## Visually off, from U11's screenshot pass

Fourteen shots in `_bmad-output/overnight/shots/`, whole-screen, at real device metrics. Status row
level with the Dynamic Island on all fourteen; nothing under the cutout or the home indicator.

1. **The PLAYTEST chip destroys text on two screens** — it completely hides the springboard's middle
   tab label, and lands mid-sentence on the lobby's `WAITING FOR EVERYONE'S LINE`.
2. **A banner slices the System Integrity meter in half** and sits over `FROZEN UNTIL NEXT HOUSE
   MEETING`; the meter is unreadable while a banner is up.
3. **Room name labels overflow their cell** in the editor (`KITCHEN`, `GARAGE`, `STAIRS`).
4. **The stairs zone counts toward the room count** — GROUND reads `6 ROOMS` including STAIRS.
   Stairs hold nothing (D-099).
5. **The room panel's MARKERS row** draws a heavy filled triangle beside a small chevron.
6. **`HomeDetail` and `Delete` are ~60% empty down the middle** at real metrics — worse than on
   desktop, where U4 first flagged it.

## One thing that is neither an escalation nor a design flag

**A repo-wide string sweep in commit `412c752` corrupted ordinary English across 50 tracked files**,
by replacing a scrubbed term without word boundaries. 49 are vendored BMAD skill files where it is
cosmetic. **The fiftieth is `_bmad-output/project-context.md:119`** — the file CLAUDE.md names as
non-negotiable and tells every agent to read first. Grep for `inpassage use`, `prepassage use` and
`reinpassage use`.

**No ruling is needed** — the `pre-commit` hook matches on word boundaries, so *inventing*,
*preventing* and *reinventing* are all perfectly lawful here and were verified to be. It is a
mechanical text fix, left for a person because a 50-file text change is outside a verification
unit's charter and because the 49 vendored skill files are better repaired by re-vendoring than by
hand. The hook skips `.claude/skills/*`, which is why nothing caught them going in. Full write-up
in **Unit 11** above.

## What is waiting for the morning

Nothing in the tree is broken and nothing is half-done. The client side of every flow built
overnight is finished and renders whatever it is pushed; **what it is waiting for is the house.**
The four meeting transitions, the Subroutine verdict, the halfway takeover and the light
signature's arrival are all loop work, and the loop is still frozen — deliberately, and with each
boundary written down above rather than guessed at.

# Morning fixes

*Owner-ruled 2026-08-21 in conversation, worked in three commits on `main`.*

| | |
|---|---|
| `fcfefd6` | The clock says forty-five, and the bar stops saying its own name |
| `76c0124` | The glass goes on, and the stairwell stops counting as a room |
| `708f41e` | The inert button stops being written out twice |

**`./gradlew check` green at each one.** Final counts, read off the result XML rather than off an
exit code: **97 files, `tests="922" failures="0" errors="0"`.**

## The scanning lines exist in this repo, and the search nearly missed them

The checklist said to stop and report rather than invent a treatment. I got as far as drafting that
report. **The reference is the device mockup itself**, checked in at
`_bmad-output/brainstorming/brainstorm-engine-selection-2026-08-15/artifacts/someone-is-home-device.html:53`,
which draws the whole panel as

```css
.screen::after{ background: repeating-linear-gradient(0deg, rgba(0,0,0,.32) 0 1px, transparent 1px 3px), … }
```

It is not in the planning artefacts, not in the GDD's art-direction section, and not in any file
with "design" in its name — `gdd.md:1540` points at it as a *reference artifact* and that is the
only thread leading there. **Worth writing down for the next person who goes looking for the
design: the mockups are the design, and they are in `brainstorming/`, not in
`planning-artifacts/`.** (The one grep hit for "scanline" outside them is
`.claude/skills/gds-ux/assets/design-example-game.md` — vendored BMAD boilerplate, and not this
project's anything.)

Transcribed unchanged: one design unit of 32% black in every three, over everything including the
banner, outside the layer that dims. The numbers are not adjusted for the phone — the mockup's
screen is about 312 CSS px against this canvas's 300 units, so a CSS pixel there and a design unit
here are the same thing to within four percent, which is the whole reason the port is written in
design units.

**Flagged, and left out:** the same CSS rule carries a second layer, a vignette
(`radial-gradient(ellipse 130% 90% at 50% 50%, transparent 55%, rgba(0,0,0,.55) 100%)`). It was not
what was asked for, and it is a gradient on a screen whose art direction says there are none. One
brush away if somebody rules for it.

**`DeviceLayoutTest` now reads uniformity per ROW rather than per region**, because the glass bands
every region by construction and a check that fires on all 112 renders is a check that gets
switched off. Injection-verified that it still sees what it was built for: a label placed in the
status row's middle zone gives `tests="1" failures="1"`, 116 faults across 120 renders. What it
cannot see is a full-width horizontal rule — already true, already deliberate, and the reason
`ruleAllowance` exists.

## The rest of the checklist

**A — the vote window is 45s** (`gdd.md:412`). `Flow.autoAdvance` said 60 000 and the lobby's row
said 60S, so each was corroborated by the other and by nothing else. **The correction had a second
consequence worth knowing about:** `Countdown.text` chose `m:ss` at a window of 60 or more, so the
moment the window became 45 the design's own drawn vote clock, `0:38`, started rendering as `38`.
The threshold was fitted to two clocks and happened to clear a third; it is now
`CLOCK_FORM_SECONDS = 30`, fitted to all four the design drew.

The host may set it, in the Insider control's presentation family. **It does not reach the wire** —
`LobbyLink` carries the Insider count because the house clamps it, and what a client may send is a
protocol decision rather than a settings row's. A test asserts it went nowhere; if the window is
ever really enforced, that test is what should fail. **Flagged:** `VOTE_WINDOWS` is 45/60/90/30 and
only the 45 is the design's. The other three are the control's range, which the design never
states — playtest's, like the `7` on the row above.

**B — the status bar no longer says SOMEONE'S HOME**, on any screen. It was on every in-play screen
and special-cased onto the Residents' win, which trained the eye to skip the one slot that later
has to deliver REVOKED or RESTRAINED.

**D — DUPLICATE is gone from Home Detail**, with `PanelActions.duplicateHome`,
`FlowModel.duplicateHome`, `SavedHomesModel.duplicateOpen` and the `baseOf` arithmetic behind
`THE BUNGALOW 2`. `freeName` has one caller and one base now.

**E1 — `HousePlanStore` and `HouseMapStore` deleted**, both halves and both tests. Their doctrine —
text in and text out, atomic writes, a failed save THROWS (D-087) — moved onto `SavedHomesStore`,
which is the only setup file there is. `houseMapStorageDirectory` became
`savedHomesStorageDirectory` and its first-launch test came with it: the create-if-absent step it
is really about is `documentPath`'s and is shared by every store in the package.

**E2 — `PanelButton` was already fixed and already guarded.** What was left was the workaround it
made obsolete: the lobby's gated LIGHTS OUT was a hand-rolled copy of `PanelButton` carrying the
comment *"that one always publishes a click action"*, which had stopped being true. Only the real
one carries the tap-target minimum, so the armed block and the inert block it is meant to be
indistinguishable from were different heights. One call now.

*Worth recording because it nearly went in as a false claim:* changing the `= null` **default** back
to `= {}` fails nothing, because every call site passes `onClick` explicitly. The behaviour that
matters is the branch, and breaking that fails three named tests —
`lightsOutIsInertUntilEveryLineIsIn`, `lockInIsPresentAndInertWithNothingChosen`,
`jamCanBeSentWithoutBeingTouchedAndAnEmptyRouteCannot`.

**E3 — `IntegrityWidget`'s KDoc** no longer claims thirty-two segments *rather than* a percentage.
It draws both, and the percentage is the disclosure boundary (D-103, revision 21) rather than a
styling choice. `PanelVals.integrityLit` said "28 of 32" and says what that is now.

**E4 — the six visual defects.**

1. **The PLAYTEST chip** moves into the home indicator's band, held to the start so it is beside
   the system's pill rather than on it. That band is the one strip `DeviceLayoutTest` asserts the
   app leaves empty, so the chip now covers nothing. Confirmed on the simulator: the springboard's
   middle dock label reads `SCAN` whole.
2. **The banner no longer slices the meter.** The content is inset by the banner's *measured*
   height — a notification is one line or three — and the inset does not track the swipe, so
   dismissing one does not drag the screen up with it.
3. **Editor room names** are bounded by their anchor stroke and ellipsised. `KITCHEN` crossing its
   own room's gridline was untidy; a long name on a one-cell room would have printed across two
   other people's rooms, and a label reaching into a room it does not name is a host about to
   stand in the wrong place in the dark.
4. **The storey count excludes stairs.** `HousePlan.roomCount` and `Floor.roomCount` count
   `RoomKind.Room`; `rooms` itself stays unfiltered, because every structural question has to see
   the stairwell. GROUND now reads `5 ROOMS`, the home `9`, of 11 painted areas.
5. **The MARKERS row**: the glyph drops to the row's summary ink — the springboard already does
   this with the same glyph in the same position — and the `›` at 7 becomes the `>` at 8 that every
   other row in the app draws.
6. **HomeDetail and Delete** draw the home's plan in the space that was a `PushDown()`, every
   storey side by side, aspect held. **Flagged as a choice, not a transcription** — no design
   covers these two screens. The argument: a host picks their house out of a list by its shape long
   before they read `2 FLOORS · 9 ROOMS`, and the screen asking them to destroy one should show
   what is about to go.

## Afternoon — Unit A1

**Outcome: done, green, pushed.** Commit **`c012109`** — *"Six marks become six names, and the line
still goes nowhere but the house"*. Full `./gradlew check`: **tests=935, failures=0, errors=0**
across 97 test-result XML files, up from **923** — this unit adds **12**. `vocabularyLint clean` on
`model`/`core`/`ui`/`app`, `redactionLint clean — 33 file(s)`, `:ui:boundaryCheck` reports
`ui clean`, **`DeviceLayoutTest` green throughout** (`tests="1" failures="0"`), and both variant
sweeps ran.

### What it is

D-115 closed E6-1, which Unit 6 raised and skipped. The lobby Unit 6 built was counts-only —
`LobbyBody.Standing` as three integers — and it said in as many words that widening it later meant
adding a field *in a diff that says so*, which is the property the two-type split (D-048, D-049)
exists for. This is that diff.

- **`LobbyBody.Naming(name)`, client → host.** A third body rather than a widened `Handover`,
  because the two arrive at different moments — a name on the way in, a line whenever its author
  has thought of one — and one body carrying both would have to be sent half-empty, which is the
  nullable-field shape rule 3 forbids. It also keeps `Handover` what its KDoc says it is: the one
  authorised exit from the phone for that text.
- **Sent on every seating, not once.** The seat waits for its token, so a resume returns to *that*
  seat — but the host's `onLost` drops the seat and its name with it, so a phone that dropped and
  came back would be an empty chip standing in the room with nothing on any screen to explain why.
  Re-announcing from memory is the cheaper half of that pair, and the name is still never stored.
- **`LobbyBody.Standing` gains `names: List<String>`** — one entry per seat, arrival order, empty
  for a phone that has said nothing. One per seat rather than the names it happens to hold, so
  `names.size` *is* `joined` and the list cannot quietly under-report a room back to a screen that
  has just counted it. It still has nowhere to put a line: not a field left blank, no field at all.
- **`LobbyDesk` holds the names in a second map beside the lines, and `standing()` reads exactly
  one of them.** That is deliberately a difference of *which map* rather than a flag on one:
  "the standing quoted a line" then takes writing `lines` where `names` is written. A leaver takes
  their name with them; `roundEnded()` wipes both.
- **The name is bounded and trimmed host-side** (`LobbyBody.Naming.LIMIT`, now the single number
  the typed field reads too). A client may send whatever it likes and a lobby screen is not where
  that gets discovered.

### The presence strip is gone, and that is the part to review

Unit 6 replaced the fixture's six named chips with a strip of marks — one per seat, `linesIn` of
them filled — as the counts made legible in the names' absence. **Both are positional.** Drawn one
above the other, the eye pairs the nth mark with the nth name and reads *who* has handed a line
over. The house never said that; it sent a count. So the strip went and the names took its place,
and the two counts stay where they were and where they cannot be paired with anybody: `6 JOINED` on
the header row, `4 OF 6 HANDED THEIRS OVER` at the foot.

The chips are all identical — no fill, no brightness, no order meaning anything beyond arrival.
**This phone's own chip is not marked**, though it could be guessed at: two people in one house may
both be a ROSE, and a screen that pointed at the wrong one is worse than a screen that points at
none. A seat nobody has spoken for reads `UNNAMED` rather than being dropped, so the list stays as
long as the count above it.

What none of this opens is **occupancy**. Who is in the game is not who is in a room, and the
meeting is what the latter is for.

### The guards, and the six bugs written to make them fail

Each injected, watched to fail in a named test, reverted by targeted reverse-replace.

| Injected | Caught by | Failure, from the XML |
|---|---|---|
| `standing()` reads `lines` where it reads `names` | `OneLineNeverPersistedTest` (iosTest, real Documents dir) + `LobbyDeskTest` + `LobbyLoopbackTest` | `1/1` *"a one line went out in the standing"*; `16/2`; `2/1` |
| `left()` keeps the name | `LobbyDeskTest` | **survived at first — see below** — then `16/1` *"somebody who has just joined is wearing the name of the player who left that seat"* |
| `roundEnded()` keeps the names | `LobbyDeskTest` | `16/1` *"a name survived the round it was given for"* |
| The lobby draws `lobby.line.text` | `LobbyInputTest` | `15/2` *"the lobby printed the one line typed on this phone"* |
| `attachTo` joins under a blank | `LobbyModelTest` + `LobbyInputTest` | `19/1` `expected:<[ELLIOT]> but was:<[]>`; `15/2` |
| The desk writes a name through `saveSavedHomes` | `OneLineNeverPersistedTest` | `1/1` *"a resident name was written to saved-homes.txt"* |

### ⚠️ One injection survived, and the test it survived was mine

`left()` keeping the name **passed** the first version of `a seat that leaves takes its name with
it`, which asserted only that the leaver was no longer in the published list. That assertion is
free: `standing()` builds from the seats, so a desk holding a stale name looks identical from
outside — *until the seat comes back*. And it does come back. `SeatLedger.release` returns a
released seat to the pool, so **the next person through the door is granted it** and walks into the
lobby wearing the name of the player who just walked out. The test now re-seats and asserts the
seat is genuinely unnamed, and the injection fails.

Worth writing down beyond this unit: a guard asserting *what is published* is not a guard on *what
is held*, and the two only diverge on the path nobody was thinking about.

### Also hardened

`OneLineNeverPersistedTest` — the real-Documents sweep with the canary positive control — now
sweeps for the **names** as well as the lines. D-115 lets a name leave the phone it was typed on;
it does not let one stay on the phone it arrived at, and the host's desk is the only thing in this
game that ever holds more than one person's. The same test also asserts the names *are* in the
standing, so a future change that quietly un-widens it fails here rather than on a screen.

`LobbyLoopbackTest` gained a resume leg over the real socket: a phone drops, comes back to the seat
its token owns, **arrives unnamed**, and is listed again only after it re-announces. That is the
property the send-on-every-seating design exists for, proved at the desk-and-wire layer.

### Flagged for a person — decisions this unit made that somebody should agree with

- **A blank name is allowed.** A player who typed nothing joins under an empty string and the lobby
  draws `UNNAMED`. The alternative — refusing to attach without a name, symmetric with
  `ONE LINE, AND MAKE IT REAL` — is a new gate on a new screen, and the host reaches the lobby by a
  path (Join → HOST A HOME INSTEAD → Maps) where the name field is behind them. Left open; it is a
  design call, not a wiring one.
- **`MemoryLobbyLink` now invents five names by default** (PRIYA, MARCUS, DANI, ROSE, TOMAS — the
  design's own lobby minus ELLIOT, which the sample phone types for itself), for the reason
  `MemoryHomeFinder` finds homes rather than nothing: a rendering test looking at an empty lobby
  proves nothing about the screen that ships.
- **`UNNAMED`** as the word on an unspoken-for chip. Not in the closed vocabulary and not near it —
  it is a state of a lobby row, not a game term — but it is a word on a screen and somebody should
  say whether it is the right one.

### Verification gap

**`LobbyOverTransport`'s re-announcement is not covered by a test.** `app` has no transport test
source (only `SeededWalkTest` and `BuildVariantTest`), so the *behaviour* — re-send `Naming` on
every `Phase.Seated` — is proved at the layer below it (`LobbyLoopbackTest`'s resume leg proves the
desk forgets and re-learns) but the seam itself is read, not run. Closing it means an `app`-level
transport test, which is a unit of its own.

### Not in this unit

**In-round name delivery.** D-115 names the vote ballot as the obvious second site — a house that
cannot say who is being Restrained cannot hold a meeting — and that is loop work. Nothing here
touches an `Event`, an `Effect`, a redaction-schema row or `reduce()`; the lobby is pre-arm
transport, which is why it was lawful under the freeze.
